\title{Minimax inference for threshold modified treatment policies with continuous treatments}

\begin{abstract}
Continuous-treatment modified treatment policies can map a range of natural treatment values to a single assigned dose. This paper studies the exact lower-threshold clamp, which sends treatments below a deterministic threshold \(\delta_n\), the policy threshold, to \(\delta_n\) and leaves larger treatments unchanged. Under a fixed finite-stratum observed-data model with bounded outcomes, Hölder outcome regression with known exponent \(\beta\) and radius \(L\), and polynomial lower-tail treatment-density thinning \(c_-a^\kappa\leq\pi_x(a)\leq c_+a^\kappa\) with known exponent \(\kappa\), the post-policy law contains a moving atom. The target is the observed clamp mean \(\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\), the sum of the retained mean above the threshold and an atom-regression product at the boundary. The paper establishes matching minimax absolute-risk and uniformly honest expected-length rates
\[
r_n=n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta,
\qquad
n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\asymp1,
\]
attained by a split-sample total-Gram stabilized local-polynomial estimator and a bias-aware interval. The phase diagram identifies the regular, critical, atom-dominated, fixed-threshold, and zero-threshold regimes induced by \(\delta_n\). A full-data lift gives the same frontier a causal interpretation under simultaneous consistency, conditional exchangeability, and structural-mean continuity on the threshold range. A continuity-only model yields the separate frontier \(n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}\), with an explicit elbow and fixed-positive-threshold behavior.
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}

Modified treatment policies define interventions by transforming the treatment value that a unit would naturally receive. This idea is central in causal analyses where feasible interventions depend on the observed treatment process, including stochastic and modified policies for continuous or longitudinal treatments \citep{Diaz2012,Haneuse2013,Young2014,DiazWilliamsHoffmanSchenck2023}. Applied implementations and tutorials make such policies operational, including threshold rules motivated by support and positivity considerations \citep{WilliamsDiaz2023lmtp,HoffmanSalazarBarretoWilliamsRudolphDiaz2024}. This paper studies one exact rule: the lower-threshold clamp \(d_\delta(a)=\max\{a,\delta\}\), which assigns all natural values below \(\delta\), the intervention threshold, to the boundary point \(\delta\).

The statistical feature of this policy is the atom created by the clamp. In a continuous-treatment experiment, values above \(\delta\) remain on their natural continuous scale, while the lower tail collapses to a point mass at \(\delta\). The corresponding observed-data target in \cref{obj:def:clamp-functional} is
\[
\theta_\delta(P)
=
\mathbb E_P\{Y\mathbf 1(A>\delta)\}
+
\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}p_xq_x(\delta)\mu_x^P(\delta),
\]
where \(q_x(\delta)\), the lower-tail conditional treatment mass, is the probability collapsed by the clamp in stratum \(x\), and \(\mu_x^P(\delta)\), the threshold regression value, is the outcome regression at the boundary. This decomposition separates an ordinary retained-course mean from an atom-regression product. The latter term is the source of the boundary inference problem.

The observed model in \cref{obj:def:model-class} fixes a finite stratum space, bounded outcomes, a Hölder regression class with known smoothness exponent \(\beta\) and radius \(L\), and a polynomial treatment-density envelope
\[
c_-a^\kappa\leq \pi_x(a)\leq c_+a^\kappa
\]
near the lower endpoint, with known thinning exponent \(\kappa\). The deterministic threshold sequence \(\delta_n\) may move with sample size. The information-balance bandwidth \(h_n\) in \cref{obj:def:bandwidth} is defined by the local balance
\[
n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\asymp1.
\]
This balance combines the usual local-polynomial bias scale \(h_n^\beta\) with the effective local design mass under polynomial thinning. Since the clamp weights the boundary regression by an atom of order \(\delta_n^{\kappa+1}\), the resulting frontier rate in \cref{obj:def:frontier} is
\[
r_n=n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta.
\]

The first main result is the minimax clamp frontier. \Cref{obj:thm:minimax-risk} establishes matching upper and lower bounds for absolute-error risk over the observed Hölder model \(\mathcal M\). The upper bound is attained by the total-Gram stabilized estimator of \cref{obj:def:total-gram-estimator}, which estimates the retained mean, the lower-tail atom masses, and the boundary regression values on separate deterministic split blocks. The local-polynomial component is used on realized designs with sufficient total Gram curvature and is paired with a bounded fallback on singular designs. The lower bound combines two same-class experiments: a global Bernoulli mean shift for the \(n^{-1/2}\) term and a localized Bernoulli perturbation at the moving threshold for the atom-weighted term.

The second main result gives honest inference at the same scale. \Cref{obj:thm:honest-length} proves that the bias-aware interval in \cref{obj:def:honest-interval} has uniform coverage over \(\mathcal M\) and worst-case expected length of order \(r_n\), and that every uniformly honest observed-sample interval has worst-case expected length at least a constant multiple of \(r_n\). The interval uses bounded-outcome concentration for the retained mean and atom-mass estimates, and a Hölder bias plus empirical weight-energy radius for the threshold regression. This connects the clamp problem to honest nonparametric confidence-interval theory \citep{Low1997NonparametricConfidenceIntervals,ArmstrongKolesar2018OptimalInference,ArmstrongKolesar2020} while preserving the atom-weighted structure of the target.

The phase diagram in \cref{obj:thm:phase-diagram} expands the compact rate \(r_n\). Two deterministic scales organize the regimes: the risk-transition scale
\[
\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}
=
n^{-1/\{2(\beta\kappa+2\beta+\kappa+1)\}},
\]
and the bandwidth-transition scale
\[
\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}
=
n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)}.
\]
Thresholds with \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to0\), or with \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to c_0\in(0,\infty)\), have root-\(n\) order. Vanishing thresholds above the critical scale enter an atom-dominated regime with rate
\[
\delta_n^{\kappa+1}(n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)}.
\]
Thresholds converging to a fixed positive value have the ordinary positive-density pointwise Hölder exponent \(\beta/(2\beta+1)\), weighted by an atom mass bounded away from zero. At threshold zero, the atom vanishes and the target reduces to the observed mean.

The causal interpretation is obtained through a full-data class tailored to the same lower-threshold policy. Members of \(\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\) in \cref{obj:def:full-data-model-class} have observed margins in \(\mathcal M\) and satisfy simultaneous structural consistency, conditional exchangeability, and continuity of the structural response mean on the threshold range. \Cref{obj:prop:causal-bridge} identifies the causal clamp mean \(\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})\) with the observed target \(\theta_\delta(P)\). \Cref{obj:prop:observed-margin-surjectivity} shows that the observed-margin map covers \(\mathcal M\) and aligns the decision criteria. Consequently, \cref{obj:thm:causal-frontier-lift} transports the same minimax risk and honest-length frontier to the causal clamp mean on \(\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\).

The paper also studies a continuity-only counterpart. In \cref{obj:def:continuity-model-class}, the density, stratum-mass, and polynomial-thinning restrictions are retained, while the regression condition supplies a memberwise continuous extension on the threshold interval. \Cref{obj:thm:continuity-only-frontier} characterizes the resulting frontier as
\[
s_n=n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}.
\]
The fallback estimator and Hoeffding interval attain this scale, and the minimax risk and honest expected length are bounded below at the same order. The elbow occurs at \(\delta_n\asymp n^{-1/[2(\kappa+1)]}\), where the collapsed atom mass reaches the sampling scale. For fixed positive thresholds, the theorem records persistent minimax risk and honest length under qualitative continuity.

The results sit at the intersection of continuous-treatment causal inference, degenerate-design regression, and honest nonparametric inference. Existing work on continuous-treatment dose-response and modified-policy estimands develops identification, efficient estimation, targeted learning, threshold-response functionals, stochastic shifts, and support-aware policy analysis under their corresponding regularity structures \citep{Imbens2000,Hirano2005,Imai2004,KennedyMaMcHughSmall2017,vanderLaanZhangGilbert2023,Diaz2026,Koo2026,McCoyZhangHubbardvanderLaanSchuler2026}. Degenerate-design regression theory gives pointwise rates when the design density vanishes near the target \citep{Gaiffas2005DegenerateDesign,Gaiffas2007Inhomogeneous}, and classical minimax and local-polynomial theory provide the positive-density benchmark \citep{Stone1982,Fan1996,Tsybakov2009}. The contribution here is the threshold-atom problem generated by the exact deterministic clamp: the local regression value is learned under thinning and then multiplied by the policy-induced atom mass.

\paragraph{Standing conditions.}
One set of conditions is in force throughout and is not repeated in the discussion surrounding each statement. The class constants \(J,\beta,L,\kappa,c_-,c_+,p_{\min},\bar\delta,\alpha\), the deterministic threshold path \((\delta_n)\), and the deterministic sample-split blocks are fixed before the sample is drawn and are available to every procedure; observations are independent and identically distributed from the observed law; the local-polynomial degree is the one induced by the smoothness exponent; and estimators and interval endpoints take values in the outcome range \([0,1]\). All uniformity statements range over laws sharing these constants, and all risks, coverage probabilities, and expected lengths are computed under the \(n\)-fold product law of the observed data. The formal statements repeat these conditions in their hypotheses because each is separately checked, but a reader may treat them as one standing regime-and-sampling condition.

Each model, target, estimator, interval, and criterion has exactly one canonical definition: the model class is \cref{obj:def:model-class}, the target is \cref{obj:def:clamp-functional}, and the estimator and interval are \cref{obj:def:total-gram-estimator,obj:def:honest-interval}. A few later environments introduce split-indexed aliases of those objects, written with an explicit block subscript, so that the finite-sample criteria can name a particular split; each such environment says which canonical definition it specializes and adds no new content. \Cref{sec:appendix-proofs-verification} is the verification supplement: it holds the auxiliary design, coercivity, and local-polynomial algebra on which the main proofs rest, and nothing in it is needed to read the main results.

Selected formal statements are proved in the verification supplement, with the precise verification scope and reproduction instructions given in \cref{sec:appendix-proofs-verification}.

The rest of the paper proceeds as follows. \Cref{sec:related-work} positions the paper within modified-policy causal inference, continuous-treatment estimation, degenerate-design regression, and honest confidence intervals. \Cref{sec:setup-assumptions} defines the observed model, clamp policy, target, bandwidth, and frontier rate. \Cref{sec:estimation-intervals} constructs the total-Gram estimator and bias-aware interval. \Cref{sec:main-statistical-results} states the minimax risk, honest-length, phase-diagram, and calibration results. \Cref{sec:causal-interpretation} gives the full-data causal lift. \Cref{sec:continuity-only} treats the continuity-only model. \Cref{sec:discussion-open-questions} discusses scope, limitations, and open questions, including exact-modulus calibration.


\section{Related work}
\label{sec:related-work}

Structural and longitudinal causal frameworks provide the counterfactual language for interventions and treatment regimes \citep{Pearl2009,Robins2000,vanderLaan2003,vanderLaan2011}. Within that language, theoretical work develops identification and estimation for stochastic and modified policies in continuous-treatment settings, including rules whose assigned dose depends on the observed natural value \citep{Diaz2012,Haneuse2013,Young2014,DiazWilliamsHoffmanSchenck2023}. Software and applied guidance make these policies operational and illustrate threshold interventions motivated by positivity and support constraints \citep{WilliamsDiaz2023lmtp,HoffmanSalazarBarretoWilliamsRudolphDiaz2024}. The present paper studies the \leanref{S-1}{observed-data experiment generated by a lower-threshold modified treatment policy} in which the deterministic rule clamps natural treatment values below the threshold to the threshold itself. That exact clamp creates a point mass at the boundary of the post-policy distribution, so the estimand combines an ordinary retained natural-course component with a boundary regression value multiplied by the mass collapsed by the policy.

A second line of work studies continuous-treatment dose-response functionals and the role of overlap in semiparametric and nonparametric inference \citep{Imbens2000,Hirano2005,Imai2004,KennedyMaMcHughSmall2017,BonviniKennedy2026}. Recent natural-value-policy work develops root-\(n\) targeted-learning inference under its regularity and nuisance-rate conditions \citep{Diaz2026}, while positivity-violation work separates an in-support point-identified component from a Lipschitz-bounded extrapolation component \citep{Koo2026}. Threshold-response methods target support-restricted stochastic functionals with efficient estimation and simultaneous bands \citep{vanderLaanZhangGilbert2023}, and stochastic-shift work targets effect modification with valid confidence intervals \citep{McCoyZhangHubbardvanderLaanSchuler2026}. The present estimand has a different post-policy law: its deterministic clamp maps the entire lower tail to one moving boundary point, producing a Dirac component whose weight and regression value must be learned together. Under a global two-sided polynomial envelope, the delivered results characterize minimax risk and uniformly honest expected length for this unsmoothed clamp functional.

The nonparametric component is closely related to regression with degenerate design. For smoothness \(s\) and a design density regularly varying with exponent \(\gamma\), \citet{Gaiffas2005DegenerateDesign} obtains the pointwise rate \(n^{-s/(1+2s+\gamma)}\), up to slowly varying factors; the classical positive-density Hölder rate \(n^{-s/(2s+1)}\) from \citet{Stone1982} and the local-polynomial constructions surveyed by \citet{Fan1996} provide the interior reference. In the present boundary regime, the information balance \(n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\asymp1\) recovers the degenerate-design bandwidth when \(\delta_n\) is at the edge scale. Farther into the interior it gives \(h_n\asymp(n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\). The clamp target then multiplies the resulting boundary-regression error by the collapsed mass of order \(\delta_n^{\kappa+1}\), yielding the atom term \(\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta\) alongside the retained-mean \(n^{-1/2}\) term. This mass weighting, absent from ordinary pointwise regression, generates the critical and atom-dominated threshold regimes.  Two antecedents deserve a closer comparison, because they bear directly on the oracle conditions used here and on the sharp-constant question left open below.

The first is adaptation under degenerate design.  \citet{Gaiffas2007Inhomogeneous} studies pointwise estimation from inhomogeneous data when the smoothness is unknown, and obtains the adaptive rate with the usual logarithmic payment: the exponent matches the known-smoothness degenerate-design rate with \(n\) replaced by \(n/\log n\), so adaptation costs a logarithmic factor rather than a change of exponent.  The procedures in the present paper are class-indexed in the oracle sense: the smoothness exponent and radius, the thinning exponent and envelope constants, and the threshold path are fixed before sampling.  Atom weighting changes the adaptation question rather than inheriting it.  The clamp target multiplies the boundary-regression error by the collapsed mass \(\delta_n^{\kappa+1}\), so a logarithmic loss in the regression component is invisible whenever the retained-mean term \(n^{-1/2}\) dominates, and is felt only above the critical threshold scale; moreover the mass itself depends on the thinning exponent, so an adaptive treatment would have to adapt to \(\kappa\) as well as to \(\beta\).  The frontier reported here is the known-class benchmark for that question: it holds with \(\beta\), \(L\), and \(\kappa\) fixed and available to the procedure, which is the reference an adaptive treatment would be measured against.  \Cref{sec:discussion-open-questions} takes up adaptation to unknown \(\beta\), \(L\), and \(\kappa\) as a question for future work.

The second is the modulus of continuity.  The classical account of minimax rates through a modulus is due to \citet{DonohoLiu1991GeometrizingII}, who show that the difficulty of estimating a linear functional over a convex class is governed by the between-class modulus of the functional with respect to a statistical distance, and \citet{CaiLow2004AdaptationTheory} develop the corresponding theory for confidence intervals, where expected length over a subclass is governed by a within-class or between-class modulus rather than by the minimax rate alone.  The honest expected-length lower bound established here is proved by direct two-point and localized-perturbation arguments, and it matches the risk frontier in order.  Relating that frontier to a modulus is a well-posed next step, and the structure of the clamp target says what such a modulus would have to accommodate: the functional is linear in the outcome regression, while its weight \(q_x(\delta)\) is determined by the design, so the appropriate family is a design-indexed one rather than the convex class of a single fixed linear functional.  The sharp-constant question stated in the discussion is exactly this calibration question.

The inference results also build on the theory of honest confidence intervals. \citet{Low1997NonparametricConfidenceIntervals} shows how expected length lower bounds constrain honest adaptation over nonparametric classes, and \citet{ArmstrongKolesar2018OptimalInference,ArmstrongKolesar2020} develop bias-aware interval methods that balance stochastic error and worst-case approximation bias. Those ideas are especially natural here because the clamp target contains a boundary regression term whose bias is controlled by smoothness and whose variance is controlled by the local design mass. The paper establishes matched minimax absolute-risk and uniformly honest expected-length rates for this target, so the estimation and interval scales agree under the same polynomial-thinning conditions.

Finally, the deterministic lower-threshold clamp studied here has a mixed-measure post-intervention law: values already above the threshold remain on their continuous natural scale, while values below the threshold collapse to the boundary point. That perspective explains why the lower-threshold clamp differs from smooth dose-response estimation: the intervention produces an atom whose mass is estimable from treatment frequencies, while its contribution to the mean depends on a boundary response value. The analysis below isolates this atom-regression product and derives the rates for both the Hölder model and the continuity-only model, thereby connecting modified-policy causal estimands to degenerate-design regression and honest nonparametric inference.


\section{Setup and assumptions}
\label{sec:setup-assumptions}

We work with deterministic sequences and fixed constants throughout. Generic finite constants may change from line to line and depend only on the displayed model parameters. All asymptotic notation is along the sample size \leanref{sym:n}{\ensuremath{n}}; indices \(x\) range over the finite stratum space \leanref{sym:mathcal_X}{\ensuremath{\mathcal X}}, and all probabilities and expectations are taken under the observed law \leanref{sym:P}{\ensuremath{P}} unless a different law is displayed. The observed unit is \leanref{sym:O}{\ensuremath{O=(X,A,Y)}}, with baseline stratum \leanref{sym:X}{\ensuremath{X}}, treatment \leanref{sym:A}{\ensuremath{A}}, and outcome \leanref{sym:Y}{\ensuremath{Y}}. The deterministic \leanref{S-2}{regime constants} include the smoothness exponent \leanref{sym:beta}{\ensuremath{\beta}}, Hölder radius \leanref{sym:L}{\ensuremath{L}}, thinning exponent \leanref{sym:kappa}{\ensuremath{\kappa}}, density constants \leanref{sym:c_minus}{\ensuremath{c_-}} and \leanref{sym:c_plus}{\ensuremath{c_+}}, stratum-mass floor \leanref{sym:p_min}{\ensuremath{p_{\min}}}, threshold envelope \leanref{sym:delta_bar}{\ensuremath{\bar\delta}}, noncoverage level \leanref{sym:alpha}{\ensuremath{\alpha}}, and deterministic threshold sequence \leanref{sym:delta_n}{\ensuremath{\delta_n}}.

The procedures below use fixed sample splitting and product sampling notation. The split is deterministic, so the probability statements condition on no random partitioning device.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_8}[Admissible three-way split]
At sample size \(n\), an admissible three-way split is a deterministic triple \(B_n=(I_0,I_1,I_2)\) of pairwise-disjoint subsets of \(\{1,\ldots,n\}\) such that \(|I_j|\geq\lfloor n/4\rfloor\) for \(j=0,1,2\). The blocks are fixed before observing the sample.
\end{definitionv}

\Cref{obj:synth_8} fixes the block structure used by the estimator and interval. The lower bounds on the block sizes keep each empirical component on the same sample-size scale while leaving the blocks independent under product sampling.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_16}[Independent product sample laws]
For an observed-data law \(P\) on \(\mathcal X\times[0,1]\times[0,1]\), write \(P^{\otimes n}\) for the \(n\)-fold product law governing the observed sample \((O_1,\ldots,O_n)\), under which the coordinates are independent with common law \(P\). For a full-data law package \(P^{\mathrm F}\), write \((P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n}\) for the \(n\)-fold product law governing \(n\) independent full-data draws from \(P^{\mathrm F}\); estimators and intervals indexed by this law are measurable functions of the induced observed coordinates \((O_1,\ldots,O_n)\).
\end{definitionv}

The notation in \cref{obj:synth_16} is used for risk and coverage statements in the observed and causal formulations. It records that full-data sampling still feeds estimators through the observed coordinates.

The sampling statement fixes the i.i.d. observed-data experiment used by the estimators and risk criteria.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:iid-sampling}[I.i.d. sampling]
The observed units \(O_1,\ldots,O_n\) are independent and identically distributed according to \(P\).
\end{assumptionv}

The independent random-design sampling condition in \cref{obj:ass:iid-sampling} is the standard sampling structure for the boundary-regression experiment \citep{Gaiffas2005DegenerateDesign}. It lets the mass above the threshold, the empirical lower-threshold atom, and the local regression fit be analyzed with product-law concentration under the same observed law.

The next conditions describe the treatment distribution within strata and the regression function at the boundary created by the lower-threshold clamp.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:conditional-density-law}[Conditional treatment density]
For each \(x\in\mathcal X\), the map \(a\mapsto \pi_x(a)\) is Borel measurable on \([0,1]\), and \(\pi_x(a)\ge 0\) for Lebesgue-a.e. \(a\in[0,1]\). Moreover, \(p_x=P(X=x)\), and for every Borel set \(B\subseteq[0,1]\),
\[
P(X=x,\ A\in B)=p_x\int_B \pi_x(a)\,\mathrm da .
\]
\end{assumptionv}

The conditional absolute-continuity requirement in \cref{obj:ass:conditional-density-law} is standard for continuous-treatment modified policies \citep{DiazWilliamsHoffmanSchenck2023}. It introduces the stratum density \leanref{sym:pi_x}{\ensuremath{\pi_x}} and the stratum mass \leanref{sym:p_x}{\ensuremath{p_x}}, so the probability collapsed by a threshold can be expressed as an ordinary integral.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:stratum-mass}[Stratum mass lower bound]
For every \(x\in\mathcal X\),
\[
p_x\geq p_{\min}.
\]
\end{assumptionv}

The finite-stratum overlap condition in \cref{obj:ass:stratum-mass} is standard in stratified continuous-treatment analyses \citep{DiazWilliamsHoffmanSchenck2023}. It ensures that every stratum contributes a nonvanishing share of the sample, which keeps the local design problem from being driven by empty baseline cells.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:polynomial-thinning}[Polynomial overlap thinning]
For every \(x\in\mathcal X\) and for Lebesgue-almost every \(a\in[0,1]\),
\[
c_- a^\kappa \leq \pi_x(a) \leq c_+ a^\kappa .
\]
\end{assumptionv}

\Cref{obj:ass:polynomial-thinning} is specific to this analysis. It states that treatment support near the lower boundary thins at a polynomial order, so the amount of information available to learn the boundary regression value is governed by the same exponent in every stratum. The two sides of the envelope do different work, and it helps to keep them apart. The lower bound \(c_-a^\kappa\le\pi_x(a)\) guarantees local information: it lower bounds the expected count and the local Gram eigenvalue in the threshold window, and it lower bounds the collapsed atom mass \(q_x(\delta)\). It is therefore the side used for Gram coercivity and for atom-mass lower bounds. The upper bound \(\pi_x(a)\le c_+a^\kappa\) controls upper-risk quantities: the same window mass, the local Gram entries, and the collapsed atom mass entering the bias and length calculations. The lower-bound experiments use the canonical density \(\pi_x(a)=(\kappa+1)a^\kappa\), which the regime constraint \(c_-\le\kappa+1\le c_+\) admits, and the likelihood divergences are computed for that canonical density and its localized perturbations. Densities proportional to \(a^\kappa\), and bounded multiplicative perturbations \(a^\kappa r_x(a)\) with \(0<c_-\le r_x(a)\le c_+\), are representative members.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_19}[Local-polynomial degree]
For each smoothness exponent \(\beta\in\mathbb R\), the local-polynomial degree is
\[
\ell(\beta):=\max\{\lceil\beta\rceil_{\mathbb N}-1,0\},
\]
where \(\lceil\beta\rceil_{\mathbb N}\) denotes the least natural number greater than or equal to \(\beta\).
\end{definitionv}

The degree \leanref{sym:ell}{\ensuremath{\ell}} in \cref{obj:synth_19} is the order used by the local polynomial at the threshold. It matches the Taylor-remainder smoothness imposed next.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:holder-regression}[Hölder regression regularity]
The regression function \(\mu_x^P\) satisfies the following condition with parameters \(\beta\) and \(L\). For every \(x\in\mathcal X\):
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Continuity.)} The map \(a\mapsto \mu_x^P(a)\) is continuous on \([0,1]\).
\item \textbf{(Range.)} For every \(a\in[0,1]\), \(\mu_x^P(a)\in[0,1]\).
\item \textbf{(Regression version.)} The function \(\mu_X^P(A)\) realizes the conditional outcome regression given the complete design:
\[
\mathbb E_P[Y\mid X,A]=\mu_X^P(A)
\quad P\text{-almost surely}.
\]
\item \textbf{(Hölder Taylor remainder.)} Let \(\ell=\ell(\beta)\) be the local-polynomial order defined in \cref{obj:synth_19}. For all \(s,t\in[0,1]\), with derivatives taken intrinsically on \([0,1]\),
\[
\left|
\mu_x^P(t)
-
\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}
\frac{(\mu_x^P)^{(j)}(s)}{j!}(t-s)^j
\right|
\le
L|t-s|^\beta .
\]
\end{itemize}
\end{assumptionv}

The Taylor-remainder formulation in \cref{obj:ass:holder-regression} is a standard Hölder outcome-regression smoothness condition for bias-aware nonparametric inference \citep{ArmstrongKolesar2020}. In this model, the boundary value of the regression \leanref{sym:mu_x_P}{\ensuremath{\mu_x^P}} is multiplied by the threshold mass, so the condition supplies the deterministic approximation control used by the local-polynomial estimator.

These ingredients define the observed model class and the lower-threshold clamp target.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_18}[Observed continuity class $\mathcal M_{\mathrm{cont}}$]
Let \(\mathcal X=\{1,\ldots,J\}\), let \(O=(X,A,Y)\in\mathcal X\times[0,1]\times[0,1]\), and let \(P\in\mathcal P(\mathcal X\times[0,1]\times[0,1])\). Write
\[
p_x=P(X=x).
\]
The law \(P\) belongs to the continuity-only class \(\mathcal M_{\mathrm{cont}}\) when, for every \(x\in\mathcal X\), the conditional-density condition of \cref{obj:ass:conditional-density-law}, the stratum-mass condition of \cref{obj:ass:stratum-mass}, and the polynomial-thinning condition of \cref{obj:ass:polynomial-thinning} hold, and an almost-everywhere version of \(a\mapsto\mathbb E_P[Y\mid A=a,X=x]\) has a unique continuous extension
\[
\mu_{x,\mathrm{cont}}^P:[0,\bar\delta]\to[0,1].
\]
The Hölder class \(\mathcal M\) imposes the same three design conditions together with the Hölder regression condition of \cref{obj:ass:holder-regression}; its canonical definition is \cref{obj:def:model-class}, and the canonical continuity-only definition is \cref{obj:def:continuity-model-class}.
\end{definitionv}

\Cref{obj:synth_18} records the continuity-only class \leanref{sym:mathcal_M_cont}{\ensuremath{\mathcal M_{\mathrm{cont}}}} alongside the Hölder class. The later continuity-only section uses the selected continuous regression extension \leanref{sym:mu_x_cont_P}{\ensuremath{\mu_{x,\mathrm{cont}}^P}} when smoothness is replaced by qualitative continuity on the threshold envelope.

\begin{definitionv}{def:model-class}[Observed law class \(\mathcal M\)]
For \(P\in\mathcal P(\mathcal X\times[0,1]\times[0,1])\), let
\[
p_x=P(X=x),
\qquad x\in\mathcal X .
\]
The observed-data law class \(\mathcal M\) is
\[
\mathcal M
=
\left\{
P\in\mathcal P(\mathcal X\times[0,1]\times[0,1])
:
P\text{ satisfies the conditions listed below}
\right\}.
\]
Membership means that \(P\) satisfies:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Conditional density.)} The conditional-density condition in \cref{obj:ass:conditional-density-law}.
\item \textbf{(Stratum mass.)} The stratum-mass condition in \cref{obj:ass:stratum-mass}.
\item \textbf{(Polynomial thinning.)} The polynomial-thinning condition in \cref{obj:ass:polynomial-thinning}.
\item \textbf{(Hölder regression.)} The Hölder member condition in \cref{obj:ass:holder-regression}.
\end{itemize}
\end{definitionv}

The observed class \leanref{sym:mathcal_M}{\ensuremath{\mathcal M}} in \cref{obj:def:model-class} collects the design and regression restrictions under one membership statement. This compact notation is the domain for the estimator, honest interval, and minimax criteria in the next two sections.

\begin{definitionv}{def:clamp-policy}[Clamp policy \(d_\delta\) and \(A^\delta\)]
For \(0\leq\delta\leq\bar\delta\), the lower-threshold clamp map \(d_\delta\) and the clamped treatment \(A^\delta\) are
\[
d_\delta(a)=\max\{a,\delta\},
\qquad
A^\delta=d_\delta(A).
\]
\end{definitionv}

The map \leanref{sym:d_delta}{\ensuremath{d_\delta}} in \cref{obj:def:clamp-policy} leaves treatments above the threshold unchanged and moves lower treatments to the threshold. The resulting target therefore combines a natural-course component above \(\delta\) with a boundary-regression component at \(\delta\).

\begin{definitionv}{def:clamp-functional}[Clamp functionals \(q_x(\delta)\), \(\nu_\delta(P)\), and \(\theta_\delta(P)\)]
For \(P\in\mathcal M\) as in \cref{obj:def:model-class}, \(x\in\mathcal X\), and \(0\leq\delta\leq\bar\delta\), define
\[
q_x(\delta)=\int_{[0,\delta]}\pi_x(a)\,\mathrm da,
\qquad
\nu_\delta(P)=\mathbb E_P\!\left[Y\mathbf 1\{A>\delta\}\right].
\]
The unsmoothed observed-data clamp target is
\[
\theta_\delta(P)
=
\nu_\delta(P)
+
\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}p_xq_x(\delta)\mu_x^P(\delta).
\]
\end{definitionv}

In \cref{obj:def:clamp-functional}, \leanref{sym:q_x}{\ensuremath{q_x(\delta)}} is the conditional probability mass collapsed by the clamp in stratum \(x\), \leanref{sym:nu_delta}{\ensuremath{\nu_\delta(P)}} is the retained mean above the threshold, and \leanref{sym:theta_delta}{\ensuremath{\theta_\delta(P)}} is the observed-data clamp target. The expression isolates the atom-regression product that drives the boundary inference problem.

At sample size \(n\) the target is written \(\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\): it is the functional of \cref{obj:def:clamp-functional} evaluated at the deterministic threshold \(\delta=\delta_n\).

The remaining definitions set the bandwidth, rate scale, and range projection used by the estimation and interval statements.

\begin{definitionv}{def:bandwidth}[Information-balance bandwidth \(h_n\)]
The information-balance bandwidth \(h_n\) for the threshold sequence \(\delta_n\) is
\[
h_n
=
\inf\left\{
h\in(0,1-\bar\delta]:
n h^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h)^\kappa\geq 1
\right\},
\]
with \(h_n=1-\bar\delta\) when the displayed set is empty.
\end{definitionv}

The bandwidth \leanref{sym:h_n}{\ensuremath{h_n}} in \cref{obj:def:bandwidth} is chosen by the local information available in a window to the right of the threshold. The factor \((\delta_n+h)^\kappa\) carries the polynomial thinning from \cref{obj:ass:polynomial-thinning} into the window-mass calculation.

\begin{definitionv}{def:frontier}[Frontier rate \(r_n\)]
The Hölder minimax rate \(r_n\) is
\[
r_n=n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta .
\]
\end{definitionv}

The rate \leanref{sym:r_n}{\ensuremath{r_n}} in \cref{obj:def:frontier} combines the regular root-\(n\) contribution from the retained mean and threshold mass with the atom-weighted Hölder approximation scale. This is the benchmark used for the minimax risk and honest expected-length results.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_6}[Projection onto the outcome range]
For a real number \(t\), define \(\Pi_{[0,1]}(t)=\min\{1,\max\{0,t\}\}\). Applied to an estimator or interval endpoint, \(\Pi_{[0,1]}\) denotes this pointwise projection onto the outcome range \([0,1]\).
\end{definitionv}

\begin{definitionv}{synth_2}[Continuity-only regression extension]
For \(P\in\mathcal M_{\mathrm{cont}}\) and \(x\in\mathcal X\), \(\mu_{x,\mathrm{cont}}^P\) denotes the unique continuous function on \([0,\bar\delta]\) that agrees, almost everywhere on that interval under the conditional treatment law given \(X=x\), with a version of the conditional mean \(a\mapsto\mathbb E_P[Y\mid A=a,X=x]\).
\end{definitionv}

\Cref{obj:synth_6} records the range projection used later for estimators and confidence endpoints. Because the target is built from an outcome regression taking values in \([0,1]\), the projection keeps the reported numerical object on the same outcome scale.


\section{Estimation and honest intervals}
\label{sec:estimation-intervals}

The estimator separates the three empirical tasks in \(\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\): estimating the retained mean above the threshold, estimating the mass collapsed by the clamp, and estimating the boundary regression value that multiplies that mass. The deterministic split from \cref{obj:synth_8} assigns one block to the retained-course mean, a second to the lower-threshold atom masses, and a third to the local-polynomial fit at the boundary. This separation lets the interval treat the three components with direct concentration bounds for bounded variables. The local regression step follows the boundary-polynomial logic of \citet{Fan1996}, with a stabilization rule tailored to the total Gram matrix in each stratum.

The realized-design notation is as follows. It records the rescaled local coordinate, the \leanref{sym:v_ell}{\ensuremath{v_\ell(u)}} monomial basis, the stratum-specific local count, and the event on which the total Gram matrix has enough curvature to support the intercept fit.

\begin{definitionv}{def:local-design-notation}[Local design notation]
For a realized sample \(z=(O_1,\ldots,O_n)\), deterministic split blocks \(I_0,I_1,I_2\) as in \cref{obj:synth_8}, a stratum \(x\in\mathcal X\), local-polynomial degree \(\ell\), threshold \(\delta_n\), and bandwidth \(h_n\), put
\[
U_i=\frac{A_i-\delta_n}{h_n},
\qquad
v_\ell(u)=(1,u,\ldots,u^\ell)^\top .
\]
Define
\[
N_x
=
\sum_{i\in I_2}\mathbf 1\{X_i=x,\ A_i\in[\delta_n,\delta_n+h_n]\}
\]
and
\[
G_x
=
\sum_{i\in I_2}
\mathbf 1\{X_i=x,\ 0\leq U_i\leq1\}
v_\ell(U_i)v_\ell(U_i)^\top .
\]
For \(\rho\geq0\), define
\[
M_{\ell,\kappa,\rho}
=
\frac{\int_0^1v_\ell(u)v_\ell(u)^\top(\rho+u)^\kappa\,\mathrm du}
{\int_0^1(\rho+u)^\kappa\,\mathrm du},
\]
and set
\[
\lambda_\star
=
\frac{c_-}{c_+}
\inf_{\rho\geq0}
\lambda_{\min}\!\left(M_{\ell,\kappa,\rho}\right).
\]
The good-design event \(\Omega_x\) is the event that \(N_x>0\) and, for every \(v\in\mathbb R^{\ell+1}\),
\[
v^\top G_xv
\geq
\frac{\lambda_\star N_x}{2}\lVert v\rVert_2^2 .
\]
The exact local-polynomial intercept weight is
\[
w_{ix}
=
\begin{cases}
\mathbf 1\{i\in I_2,\ X_i=x,\ 0\leq U_i\leq1\}\,
e_0^\top G_x^{-1}v_\ell(U_i),
& \text{on }\Omega_x,\\
0, & \text{on }\Omega_x^c .
\end{cases}
\]
\end{definitionv}

In \cref{obj:def:local-design-notation}, the local count \leanref{sym:N_x}{\ensuremath{N_x}} and the total localized Gram matrix \leanref{sym:G_x}{\ensuremath{G_x}} are computed on the third split block \leanref{sym:I_2}{\ensuremath{I_2}}. The normalized population moment matrix \leanref{sym:M_kappa_rho}{\ensuremath{M_{\ell,\kappa,\rho}}} supplies the reference curvature induced by the polynomial thinning law in \cref{obj:ass:polynomial-thinning}. The constant \leanref{sym:lambda_star}{\ensuremath{\lambda_\star}} converts that reference curvature into a uniform empirical design threshold, and the good-design event \leanref{sym:Omega_x}{\ensuremath{\Omega_x}} is the realized condition under which the intercept weights \leanref{sym:w_ix}{\ensuremath{w_{ix}}} are used.

With these quantities fixed, the point estimator is a plug-in estimator for the decomposition in \cref{obj:def:clamp-functional}. It estimates each summand on its own split block and applies the range projection introduced in \cref{obj:synth_6}.

\begin{algorithmv}{def:total-gram-estimator}[Total-Gram estimator \(\widehat\theta_n\)]
Given split blocks \(I_0,I_1,I_2\) and the local-design quantities in \cref{obj:def:local-design-notation}, define the estimator as follows.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute the retained-course empirical mean
\[
\widehat\nu_n
=
\frac{1}{|I_0|}
\sum_{i\in I_0}Y_i\mathbf 1\{A_i>\delta_n\}.
\]
\item For each \(x\in\mathcal X\), compute the empirical lower-threshold atom mass
\[
\widehat b_x
=
\frac{1}{|I_1|}
\sum_{i\in I_1}\mathbf 1\{X_i=x,\ A_i\leq\delta_n\}.
\]
\item For each \(x\in\mathcal X\), compute the clipped local-polynomial threshold estimate
\[
\widetilde\mu_x
=
\begin{cases}
\Pi_{[0,1]}\!\left(\sum_{i\in I_2}w_{ix}Y_i\right),& \Omega_x,\\
1/2,& \Omega_x^{c}.
\end{cases}
\]
\item Output the total-Gram clamp-target estimator
\[
\widehat\theta_n
=
\Pi_{[0,1]}\!\left[
\widehat\nu_n+\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}\widehat b_x\widetilde\mu_x
\right].
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{algorithmv}

The estimator \leanref{sym:theta_hat_n}{\ensuremath{\widehat\theta_n}} in \cref{obj:def:total-gram-estimator} uses the local-polynomial intercept only on \(\Omega_x\). On the complementary realized designs, the fallback value \(1/2\) keeps the atom-regression factor inside the bounded outcome range. The projection in the last line keeps the estimator on the same scale as \(\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\).

For statements indexed by a full deterministic split \(B_n\), it is useful to record the same construction with the split made explicit.

\begin{algorithmv}{def:honest-interval}[Honest interval \(\mathrm{CI}_n\)]
Given the local-design quantities in \cref{obj:def:local-design-notation}, define the interval as follows.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Set the Hoeffding calibration multiplier and split-block stochastic-error radii
\[
t_\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{\log(12J/\alpha)}{2}},
\qquad
b_0=\frac{t_\alpha}{\sqrt{|I_0|}},
\qquad
b_1=\frac{t_\alpha}{\sqrt{|I_1|}}.
\]
\item For each \(x\in\mathcal X\), set the local regression bias-plus-noise radius
\[
B_x
=
Lh_n^\beta\sum_{i\in I_2}|w_{ix}|
+
t_\alpha\left(\sum_{i\in I_2}w_{ix}^2\right)^{1/2}.
\]
\item For each \(x\in\mathcal X\), set the atom-weighted interval contribution
\[
D_x
=
\begin{cases}
(\widehat b_x+b_1)B_x+b_1,& \Omega_x,\\
\widehat b_x+b_1,& \Omega_x^{c}.
\end{cases}
\]
\item Output the bias-aware honest interval
\[
\mathrm{CI}_n
=
\left[
\widehat\theta_n-b_0-\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}D_x,\,
\widehat\theta_n+b_0+\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}D_x
\right]\cap[0,1].
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{algorithmv}

\Cref{obj:synth_4} is the version used by the finite-sample risk and length criteria below. It makes explicit that the sampling split is fixed before the data are observed, while the good-design events and intercept weights are realized from the third block.

The confidence interval adds deterministic bias allowances to concentration radii. The stochastic terms use Hoeffding-type calibration for bounded outcomes and indicators \citep{Hoeffding1963}, while the local regression radius combines a Hölder bias bound with the empirical \(\ell_2\) size of the intercept weights. This bias-aware construction is in the spirit of honest nonparametric inference with smoothness restrictions \citep{ArmstrongKolesar2018OptimalInference,ArmstrongKolesar2020}.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_4}[Total-Gram stabilized estimator]
Let \(B_n=(I_{0,n},I_{1,n},I_{2,n})\) be a deterministic admissible three-way split, let \(h_n\) be the information-balance bandwidth, and let \(\ell\) be the local-polynomial order. Define the projection
\[
  \operatorname{clamp}_{[0,1]}(t)=\min\{1,\max\{0,t\}\}.
\]
Set
\[
  \widehat\nu_n=|I_{0,n}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{0,n}}Y_i\mathbf 1\{A_i>\delta_n\},\qquad
  \widehat b_x=|I_{1,n}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{1,n}}\mathbf 1\{X_i=x,A_i\le\delta_n\}.
\]
For each \(x\), let \(\widetilde\mu_x=\operatorname{clamp}_{[0,1]}(\sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}w_{ix}Y_i)\) on \(\Omega_x\), and let \(\widetilde\mu_x=1/2\) on \(\Omega_x^c\). The split-indexed total-Gram estimator is
\[
  \widehat\theta_{n,B_n}^{\mathrm{TG}}
  =\operatorname{clamp}_{[0,1]}\!\left(\widehat\nu_n+\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}\widehat b_x\widetilde\mu_x\right).
\]
\end{definitionv}

Here \leanref{sym:J}{\ensuremath{J}}, the number of strata, enters only through the Hoeffding multiplier \leanref{sym:t_alpha}{\ensuremath{t_\alpha}}. The interval \leanref{sym:CI_n}{\ensuremath{\mathrm{CI}_n}} in \cref{obj:def:honest-interval} treats the retained mean, atom masses, and local-regression intercepts as separate empirical components. On \(\Omega_x\), \(D_x\) carries both the uncertainty in \(\widehat b_x\) and the bias-plus-noise radius for the threshold regression. On \(\Omega_x^c\), the contribution covers the full feasible atom-regression range, which is the branch used to preserve the same bounded-outcome scale on singular realized designs.

The split-indexed interval records the same accounting with \(B_n\) attached to each empirical quantity.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_12}[Bias-aware interval for a fixed split $B_n$]
Let $B_n=(I_{0,n},I_{1,n},I_{2,n})$ be an admissible deterministic three-way split at sample size $n$. With $h_n$ and $\delta_n$ fixed, form the total-Gram weights $w_{ix}$ and good-design events $\Omega_x$ from the third block $I_{2,n}$. Set $\widehat\nu_{n,B_n}=|I_{0,n}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{0,n}}Y_i\mathbf 1\{A_i>\delta_n\}$, $\widehat b_{x,B_n}=|I_{1,n}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{1,n}}\mathbf 1\{X_i=x,A_i\leq\delta_n\}$, and $\widetilde\mu_{x,B_n}=\Pi_{[0,1]}(\sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}w_{ix}Y_i)$ on $\Omega_x$, with $\widetilde\mu_{x,B_n}=1/2$ on $\Omega_x^c$. Define $\widehat\theta^{\mathrm{TG}}_{n,B_n}=\Pi_{[0,1]}\{\widehat\nu_{n,B_n}+\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}\widehat b_{x,B_n}\widetilde\mu_{x,B_n}\}$. Let $t_\alpha=\sqrt{\log(12J/\alpha)/2}$, $b_{0,B_n}=t_\alpha/\sqrt{|I_{0,n}|}$, and $b_{1,B_n}=t_\alpha/\sqrt{|I_{1,n}|}$. For each $x\in\mathcal X$, set $B_{x,B_n}=Lh_n^\beta\sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}|w_{ix}|+t_\alpha(\sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}w_{ix}^2)^{1/2}$ and $D_{x,B_n}=(\widehat b_{x,B_n}+b_{1,B_n})B_{x,B_n}+b_{1,B_n}$ on $\Omega_x$, while $D_{x,B_n}=\widehat b_{x,B_n}+b_{1,B_n}$ on $\Omega_x^c$. The bias-aware interval formed from $B_n$ is $\mathrm{CI}_{n,B_n}=[\widehat\theta^{\mathrm{TG}}_{n,B_n}-b_{0,B_n}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}D_{x,B_n},\widehat\theta^{\mathrm{TG}}_{n,B_n}+b_{0,B_n}+\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}D_{x,B_n}]\cap[0,1]$.
\end{definitionv}

\Cref{obj:synth_12} fixes the interval whose coverage and expected length are evaluated in the main statistical results. Its two branches mirror \cref{obj:def:honest-interval}: the regular branch combines atom-mass uncertainty with local-polynomial bias and noise, and the fallback branch uses the bounded outcome range when the realized design lacks sufficient Gram curvature.

The length criterion is pointwise in the observed sample and then averaged under the observed product law.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_17}[Length of an observed-sample interval]
For an observed-sample measurable interval \(C_n=C_n(O_1,\ldots,O_n)=[\ell_n(O_1,\ldots,O_n),u_n(O_1,\ldots,O_n)]\subseteq[0,1]\), define its length pointwise by \(\operatorname{len}(C_n)=u_n(O_1,\ldots,O_n)-\ell_n(O_1,\ldots,O_n)\). Thus expected lengths such as \(\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}[\operatorname{len}(C_n)]\) are taken with respect to the \(n\)-fold observed-sample law.
\end{definitionv}

Finally, the finite-sample criteria place the split-indexed estimator and interval on the common worst-case scale used in the minimax comparisons.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_5}[Concrete stabilized risk and length]
For a deterministic admissible split \(B_n\), define the finite-sample worst-case risk of the total-Gram estimator by
\[
  \operatorname{Risk}_n(B_n)=\sup_{P\in\mathcal M}
  \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left|\widehat\theta_{n,B_n}^{\mathrm{TG}}-\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\right|.
\]
For the bias-aware interval \(\mathrm{CI}_{n,B_n}\) formed from the same split, define its worst-case expected length by
\[
  \operatorname{Length}_n(B_n)=\sup_{P\in\mathcal M}
  \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}[\operatorname{len}(\mathrm{CI}_{n,B_n})].
\]
\end{definitionv}

\Cref{obj:synth_5} evaluates the point estimator and interval over the observed-law class \(\mathcal M\) from \cref{obj:def:model-class}. The risk uses absolute error for the clamp target \(\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\), and the length functional applies \cref{obj:synth_17} to the bias-aware interval from \cref{obj:synth_12}.


\section{Main statistical results}
\label{sec:main-statistical-results}

\Cref{sec:estimation-intervals} constructed the split-sample total-Gram estimator and its bias-aware interval for the observed clamp target in \cref{obj:def:clamp-functional}. We now state the corresponding performance guarantees. The product sampling law in \cref{obj:synth_16} and the interval-length convention in \cref{obj:synth_17} put point estimators, confidence intervals, and minimax benchmarks on the same finite-sample scale.

The scalar calibration at the end of the section uses a concrete Bernoulli pair. We record it before the main bounds so that the same notation can be used in the rate check.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_10}[One-cell Bernoulli regressions]
In the one-stratum calibration with \(\beta=\kappa=1\) and \(\pi(a)=2a\), define the null Bernoulli regression by \(m_0(a)=1/2\). For a threshold \(\delta_n\) and bandwidth \(h_n>0\), define the localized alternative by \(m_{1,\delta_n,h_n}(a)=1/2+h_n\,\tau_{\delta_n,h_n}(a)\), where \(\tau_{\delta,h}(a)=\max\{0,1-|a-\delta|/h\}\).
\end{definitionv}

The triangular alternative in \cref{obj:synth_10} is centered at the moving threshold and has height proportional to the bandwidth. It gives a transparent version of the same local perturbation that drives the lower-bound component of the general theory.

The minimax benchmarks optimize over all observed-sample procedures. The first criterion measures worst-case absolute accuracy for estimating the unsmoothed clamp target, and the second measures worst-case expected length among intervals with uniform coverage.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_3}[Observed minimax risk and honest length]
For the observed clamp target \(\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\) over \(\mathcal M\), the minimax absolute-error risk is
\[
  R_n^\star=\inf_{\widehat\theta}\sup_{P\in\mathcal M}
  \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left|\widehat\theta(O_1,\ldots,O_n)-\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\right|,
\]
where the infimum ranges over observed-sample measurable estimators taking values in \([0,1]\). The minimax honest expected length is
\[
  L_n^\star=\inf_{C_n:\,\inf_{P\in\mathcal M}P^{\otimes n}\{\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\in C_n\}\ge 1-\alpha}
  \sup_{P\in\mathcal M}\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}[\operatorname{len}(C_n)],
\]
where \(C_n\) ranges over observed-sample measurable interval procedures with endpoints in \([0,1]\).
\end{definitionv}

Thus \leanref{sym:R_n_star}{\ensuremath{R_n^\star}} is the best possible worst-case absolute accuracy for the observed clamp target, while \leanref{sym:L_n_star}{\ensuremath{L_n^\star}} is the best possible worst-case length under uniform honesty. These criteria match the honesty framework used in nonparametric confidence intervals \citep{Low1997NonparametricConfidenceIntervals,ArmstrongKolesar2018OptimalInference}.

The main risk result gives matching upper and lower bounds at the rate in \cref{obj:def:frontier}. The upper bound is attained by the stabilized total-Gram estimator from \cref{obj:def:total-gram-estimator}; the lower bound is witnessed by both a global mean shift and a localized threshold perturbation.

\begin{theoremv}{thm:minimax-risk}[Minimax clamp frontier]
Fix \(J,\beta,\kappa,L,c_-,c_+,p_{\min},\bar\delta,\alpha\) satisfying the regime conditions:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Regime.)} \(J\geq 1\), \(\beta>0\), \(\kappa\geq0\), \(L>0\), \(c_->0\), \(c_-\leq \kappa+1\leq c_+\), \(p_{\min}>0\), \(p_{\min}\leq 1/J\), \(\bar\delta\in(0,1)\), and \(\alpha\in(0,1/2)\).
\end{itemize}
Then there exist constants \(0<c<C\) and an amplitude \(a\in(0,1/4]\), depending only on the displayed regime constants, such that, for every deterministic threshold sequence \((\delta_n)_{n\geq1}\) with \(\delta_n\in[0,\bar\delta]\) for every \(n\) and every deterministic sequence \(B_n=(I_{0,n},I_{1,n},I_{2,n})\) of three pairwise-disjoint sample blocks with \(|I_{j,n}|\geq \lfloor n/4\rfloor\) for \(j=0,1,2\), for all sufficiently large \(n\), with \(h_n\) the information-balance bandwidth from \cref{obj:def:bandwidth} and
\[
r_n=n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta
\]
as in \cref{obj:def:frontier}, the observed minimax absolute-error risk \(R_n^\star\) over the clamp model \(\mathcal M\) in \cref{obj:def:model-class} and the worst-case i.i.d. risk over \(\mathcal M\) of the stabilized estimator using \(B_n\) satisfy
\[
c r_n
\leq
R_n^\star
\leq
\sup_{P\in\mathcal M} \mathbb E_P\!\left[
\left|\widehat\theta_{n,B_n}^{\mathrm{TG}}-\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\right|
\right]
\leq
C r_n .
\]
Moreover, for all sufficiently large \(n\), the lower bound is witnessed inside the same clamp model by:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Global shift.)} Laws \(P_0,P_1\in\mathcal M\) satisfying \cref{obj:ass:iid-sampling}, with the same \((X,A)\)-design distribution and the same \(p_x\) and \(\pi_x\) functions, having Bernoulli outcomes, and obeying
\[
\mu_x^{P_1}(a)=\mu_x^{P_0}(a)+n^{-1/2}
\quad\text{for every }x\text{ and }a\in[0,1],
\]
with well-posed product chi-squared divergence,
\[
P_1^{\otimes n}\ll P_0^{\otimes n},
\qquad
\int \left(\frac{dP_1^{\otimes n}}{dP_0^{\otimes n}}-1\right)^2\,dP_0^{\otimes n}<\infty,
\]
and
\[
c n^{-1/2}
\leq
\left|\theta_{\delta_n}(P_1)-\theta_{\delta_n}(P_0)\right|,
\qquad
\chi^2\!\left(P_1^{\otimes n},P_0^{\otimes n}\right)\leq C .
\]
\item \textbf{(Localized perturbation.)} Laws \(Q_0,Q_1\in\mathcal M\) satisfying \cref{obj:ass:iid-sampling}, with the same \((X,A)\)-design distribution and the same \(p_x\) and \(\pi_x\) functions, having Bernoulli outcomes, and admitting a continuous bump \(b:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) satisfying \(b(0)=1\), \(b(u)\geq0\) for every \(u\), \(b(u)=0\) for \(u\notin[-1,1]\), and \(|b(u)|\leq1\) for \(u\in[-1,1]\), with regression shift
\[
\mu_x^{Q_1}(t)-\mu_x^{Q_0}(t)
=
a\,h_n^\beta b\!\left(\frac{t-\delta_n}{h_n}\right)
\]
for every \(x\) and \(t\in[0,1]\). The product chi-squared divergence is well posed,
\[
Q_1^{\otimes n}\ll Q_0^{\otimes n},
\qquad
\int \left(\frac{dQ_1^{\otimes n}}{dQ_0^{\otimes n}}-1\right)^2\,dQ_0^{\otimes n}<\infty,
\]
and
\[
c\,\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta
\leq
\left|\theta_{\delta_n}(Q_1)-\theta_{\delta_n}(Q_0)\right|,
\qquad
\chi^2\!\left(Q_1^{\otimes n},Q_0^{\otimes n}\right)\leq C .
\]
\end{itemize}
\end{theoremv}

The two lower-bound constructions in \cref{obj:thm:minimax-risk} isolate the two terms in \(r_n\). A global Bernoulli shift produces the parametric contribution, while a Hölder-sized bump at the threshold produces the atom-weighted local contribution. The estimator in \cref{obj:def:total-gram-estimator} attains this combined scale, so the observed clamp problem has the same order from the attainable and unavoidable sides.

The corresponding interval result uses the same rate scale. Its statement combines finite-sample coverage for the constructed interval with a minimax lower bound for every honest observed-sample interval.

\begin{theoremv}{thm:honest-length}[Honest length frontier]
There are constants \(c,C\in(0,\infty)\), with \(c<C\), for which the following holds.

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Regime constants.)} The number of strata \(J\) is a positive integer, \(\beta>0\), \(\kappa\geq0\), \(L>0\), \(c_->0\), \(c_-\leq \kappa+1\leq c_+\), \(p_{\min}>0\), \(p_{\min}\leq 1/J\), \(\bar\delta\in(0,1)\), and \(\alpha\in(0,1/2)\).
\item \textbf{(Threshold path.)} The deterministic thresholds satisfy \(0\leq \delta_n\leq \bar\delta\) for every \(n\).
\item \textbf{(Sample splits.)} For every \(n\), \(B_n=(I_{0,n},I_{1,n},I_{2,n})\) is a deterministic three-way split of the sample indices with pairwise disjoint blocks and \(|I_{j,n}|\geq \lfloor n/4\rfloor\) for \(j=0,1,2\).
\end{itemize}

For all sufficiently large \(n\), set \(h_n\) to be the information-balance bandwidth for \(\delta_n\) from \cref{obj:def:bandwidth}, and set
\[
r_n=n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta .
\]
Then the stabilized interval \(\mathrm{CI}_n\) has worst-case coverage
\[
\inf\Bigl\{
P^{\otimes n}\{\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\in \mathrm{CI}_n(O_1,\ldots,O_n)\}:
P\in\mathcal M
\Bigr\}\geq 1-\alpha,
\]
and its worst-case expected length is comparable to \(r_n\):
\[
c\,r_n
\leq
\sup\Bigl\{
\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\bigl[\operatorname{len}(\mathrm{CI}_n)\bigr]:
P\in\mathcal M
\Bigr\}
\leq
C\,r_n .
\]
Moreover, the minimax honest expected length satisfies
\[
c\,r_n
\leq
L_n^\star,
\]
where \(L_n^\star\) is the infimum, over observed-sample confidence procedures with uniform coverage at least \(1-\alpha\) over \(\mathcal M\), of their worst-case expected length. Equivalently, every observed-sample confidence procedure \(C_n\) with
\[
\inf\Bigl\{
P^{\otimes n}\{\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\in C_n(O_1,\ldots,O_n)\}:
P\in\mathcal M
\Bigr\}\geq 1-\alpha
\]
has worst-case expected length at least \(c\,r_n\):
\[
c\,r_n
\leq
\sup\Bigl\{
\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\bigl[\operatorname{len}(C_n)\bigr]:
P\in\mathcal M
\Bigr\}.
\]
\end{theoremv}

The interval in \cref{obj:thm:honest-length} is calibrated to the same two sources of difficulty as the estimator. The retained-course and atom-mass terms are controlled by bounded-outcome concentration, while the threshold regression term carries the local Hölder bias and the realized Gram behavior encoded in \cref{obj:def:honest-interval}. The lower bound shows that uniform honesty over \(\mathcal M\) requires expected length of order \(r_n\), in line with modulus-based accounts of optimal confidence sets \citep{Low1997NonparametricConfidenceIntervals,ArmstrongKolesar2020}.

The two theorems above leave the rate in the compact form \(r_n\). The next result expands that expression into the threshold regimes induced by the information-balance bandwidth. Two deterministic scales organize those regimes, the risk-transition scale \leanref{sym:delta_crit}{\ensuremath{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}} and the bandwidth-transition scale \leanref{sym:delta_edge}{\ensuremath{\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}}},
\[
\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}
=
n^{-1/\{2(\beta\kappa+2\beta+\kappa+1)\}},
\qquad
\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}
=
n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)} ,
\]
and they are restated inside \cref{obj:thm:phase-diagram}. \Cref{tab:phase-summary} collects the regimes, separating the geometry of the treatment support at the threshold from the resulting risk order.

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{p{0.11\linewidth}p{0.25\linewidth}p{0.25\linewidth}p{0.26\linewidth}}
\hline
Result type & Condition & Treatment-support geometry & Conclusion \\
\hline
Bandwidth & \(\delta_n=O(\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n})\) & window reaches the thinned endpoint \(a=0\) & \(h_n\asymp n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)}\). \\
Bandwidth & \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\to\infty\) & window sits away from the thinned endpoint & \(h_n\asymp (n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\). \\
Risk & \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to0\) & threshold near the thinned endpoint & retained mean dominates: \(r_n\asymp n^{-1/2}\). \\
Risk & \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to c_0\in(0,\infty)\) & threshold near the thinned endpoint & retained mean and atom term balance: \(r_n\asymp n^{-1/2}\). \\
Risk & \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to\infty\) and \(\delta_n\to0\) & threshold vanishing but above the edge scale & atom term dominates: \(r_n\asymp \delta_n^{\kappa+1}(n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)}\). \\
Risk & \(\delta_n\to\delta_0\in(0,\bar\delta]\) & threshold interior to the treatment support & atom-weighted pointwise Hölder rate: \(r_n\asymp n^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)}\). \\
Risk & \(\delta_n=0\) & no mass collapsed & \(r_n=n^{-1/2}\). \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Bandwidth and risk regimes for the lower-threshold clamp, with \(\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}=n^{-1/\{2(\beta\kappa+2\beta+\kappa+1)\}}\) and \(\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}=n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)}\). The third column records the geometry of the treatment support at the threshold; the fourth records which contribution determines the risk order.}
\label{tab:phase-summary}
\end{table}

\begin{theoremv}{thm:phase-diagram}[Phase boundary regimes]
Let \(J\) be a positive integer and let
\[
\beta>0,\qquad
\kappa\geq 0,\qquad
L>0,\qquad
0<c_-\leq \kappa+1\leq c_+,\qquad
0<p_{\min}\leq 1/J,\qquad
0<\bar\delta<1,\qquad
0<\alpha<1/2 .
\]
Define the phase and edge scales by
\[
\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}
=
n^{-1/\{2(\beta\kappa+2\beta+\kappa+1)\}},
\qquad
\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}
=
n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)} .
\]
Then
\[
\frac{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}{\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}}\to\infty .
\]
Moreover, for every deterministic threshold sequence \((\delta_n)_{n\geq 1}\) with \(\delta_n\in[0,\bar\delta]\) for every \(n\), let \(h_n\) be the information-balance bandwidth in \cref{obj:def:bandwidth}, and set
\[
a_n=\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta,
\qquad
r_n=n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta
\]
as in \cref{obj:def:frontier}. The following conclusions hold:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Regular thresholds.} If \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to 0\), then \(r_n\asymp n^{-1/2}\).
\item \textbf{Critical thresholds.} For every \(c_0>0\), if \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to c_0\), then \(a_n\asymp n^{-1/2}\) and \(r_n\asymp n^{-1/2}\).
\item \textbf{Vanishing atom-dominated thresholds.} If \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to\infty\) and \(\delta_n\to0\), then
\[
r_n\asymp
\delta_n^{\kappa+1}\bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)} .
\]
\item \textbf{Fixed thresholds.} For every \(\delta_0\in(0,\bar\delta]\), if \(\delta_n\to\delta_0\), then
\[
r_n\asymp n^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)} .
\]
\item \textbf{Zero threshold identities.} For every \(n\) and every \(P\in\mathcal M\) satisfying \cref{obj:def:model-class},
\[
q_x(0)=0\quad\text{for every }x,\qquad
\theta_0(P)=\int Y\,dP,\qquad
r_n\big|_{\delta_n=0}=n^{-1/2},
\]
where the last identity uses the bandwidth \(h_n\) at threshold \(0\).
\item \textbf{Zero threshold estimator.} For every \(n\), every \(P\in\mathcal M\) satisfying \cref{obj:def:model-class}, and every admissible three-way split \(B=(I_0,I_1,I_2)\) as in \cref{obj:def:total-gram-estimator}, the total-Gram estimator in \cref{obj:def:total-gram-estimator}, formed at threshold \(0\) with bandwidth \(h_n\), agrees under the product sampling law generated by \(P\) with
\[
z\mapsto
\operatorname{clamp}_{[0,1]}\!\left(\frac{1}{|I_0|}\sum_{i\in I_0}Y_i\right).
\]
\item \textbf{Zero threshold stabilized guarantees.} For every deterministic sequence \(B_\bullet=(B_n)_{n\geq1}\) of admissible three-way split blocks, the stabilized zero-threshold procedure has coverage at least \(1-\alpha\) for all sufficiently large \(n\). Its stabilized worst-case risk at threshold \(0\) and its stabilized worst-case expected length at threshold \(0\) with noncoverage level \(\alpha\) are both asymptotic to \(n^{-1/2}\).
\end{itemize}
\end{theoremv}

\Cref{obj:thm:phase-diagram} identifies \(\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\) as the boundary at which the local atom contribution reaches the parametric scale, and \(\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\) as the smaller edge scale for the bandwidth algebra. Regular and critical threshold paths retain root-\(n\) order. Vanishing thresholds above the critical scale inherit a degenerate-design nonparametric rate of the kind studied by \citet{Gaiffas2005DegenerateDesign}: the estimation point rides toward the thinned endpoint \(a=0\), so the local design mass degenerates at the polynomial rate implied by \cref{obj:ass:polynomial-thinning}. A fixed positive threshold is an interior-design geometry. There the target point \(\delta_0\) is interior to the treatment support and the density at that point is bounded away from zero, so the exponent \(\beta/(2\beta+1)\) is the ordinary positive-density pointwise Hölder rate \citep{Stone1982,Donoho1991} multiplied by an atom weight \(\delta_0^{\kappa+1}\) bounded away from zero. The estimator still uses a one-sided window, because the clamp evaluates the regression at \(\delta_0\) from above, but that is a property of the procedure rather than of the design geometry. At the exact zero threshold, the clamp target reduces to the observed mean and the total-Gram estimator reduces to the clipped split-sample outcome mean.

The section closes with a scalar calibration. It specializes the localized perturbation to one stratum, linear thinning, and Lipschitz smoothness, giving an explicit exponent check for the critical scale.

\begin{propositionv}{prop:one-cell-calibration}[One-cell calibration]
Let \(\bar\delta\in(0,1)\), and let \((\delta_n)_{n\geq1}\) be a deterministic threshold sequence satisfying
\[
0\leq \delta_n\leq \bar\delta
\qquad\text{for every }n .
\]
Assume that \(\delta_n\gg n^{-1/4}\), in the sense that
\[
\frac{\delta_n}{n^{-1/4}}\longrightarrow\infty .
\]
In the one-stratum model with \(\beta=\kappa=1\) and \(\pi(a)=2a\), let \(h_n\) be the information-balance bandwidth from \cref{obj:def:bandwidth} evaluated at \((\delta_n,1,1,\bar\delta)\). For the concrete localized Bernoulli alternatives, define
\[
K_n
=
n\int_0^1
\operatorname{KL}\!\left(
\operatorname{Bernoulli}(m_{1,\delta_n,h_n}(a)),
\operatorname{Bernoulli}(m_0(a))
\right)\,2a\,da
\]
and
\[
\Delta_n
=
\int_{\delta_n}^1
\bigl(m_{1,\delta_n,h_n}(a)-m_0(a)\bigr)\,2a\,da
+
\left(\int_0^{\delta_n}2a\,da\right)
\bigl(m_{1,\delta_n,h_n}(\delta_n)-m_0(\delta_n)\bigr).
\]
Then
\[
h_n\asymp (n\delta_n)^{-1/3},
\qquad
K_n\asymp n\delta_n h_n^3,
\qquad
\Delta_n\asymp \delta_n^2h_n.
\]
Moreover,
\[
\delta_n\asymp n^{-1/10}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\Delta_n\asymp n^{-1/2}.
\]
\end{propositionv}

In \cref{obj:prop:one-cell-calibration}, \(K_n\) is the product Kullback--Leibler scale for the localized Bernoulli alternative, while \(\Delta_n\) is the induced clamp-target separation. The balance \(h_n\asymp(n\delta_n)^{-1/3}\) keeps the localized alternative statistically contiguous at constant information scale, and the identity \(\delta_n\asymp n^{-1/10}\) reproduces the critical exponent from \cref{obj:thm:phase-diagram} when \(\beta=\kappa=1\).


\section{Causal interpretation through full-data lifts}
\label{sec:causal-interpretation}

The observed-data results in \cref{obj:thm:minimax-risk,obj:thm:honest-length} concern the clamp functional \(\theta_\delta(P)\) under the law of \((X,A,Y)\). This section supplies a full-data causal interpretation for the same target.  One notational convention applies throughout this section and the continuity-only section.  Every estimator and interval used in this paper is a measurable function of the observed sample, so its risk, coverage probability, and expected length are the same whether computed under the \(n\)-fold product law of the observed margin or under the \(n\)-fold product law of the full-data package.  We therefore state all such quantities under the observed-margin product law \(P^{\otimes n}\), and use the full-data product law only where the statement genuinely concerns full-data objects. The structural full-data overlay augments the observed experiment with a full-data law \leanref{sym:P_full}{\ensuremath{P^{\mathrm F}}}, a member-specific latent-response space \leanref{sym:mathcal_U}{\ensuremath{\mathcal U}}, a latent response variable \leanref{sym:U}{\ensuremath{U}}, structural response maps \leanref{sym:g_x}{\ensuremath{g_x}}, and a potential-outcome process \leanref{sym:Y_a}{\ensuremath{\{Y(a):a\in[0,1]\}}}. Within that overlay, the lower-threshold policy changes the realized treatment through the same clamp map introduced in \cref{obj:def:clamp-policy}; the statistical analysis remains based on the observed sample.

The analysis rests on three full-data restrictions and one response-mean definition. The first two restrictions are standard causal conditions for a latent-response representation, and the third records the continuity required on the threshold interval used by the clamp policy.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:consistency}[Structural consistency]
Under \(P^{\mathrm F}\), the following conditions hold:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Latent response.)} The latent response variable \(U\) takes values in \(\mathcal U\).
\item \textbf{(Structural maps.)} For every \(x\in\mathcal X\), the map
\[
g_x:[0,1]\times\mathcal U\to[0,1]
\]
is jointly Borel measurable.
\item \textbf{(Potential and observed outcomes.)} There is a single \(P^{\mathrm F}\)-null set outside which, for every \(a\in[0,1]\),
\[
Y(a)=g_X(a,U),
\qquad
Y=g_X(A,U).
\]
\end{itemize}
\end{assumptionv}

\Cref{obj:ass:consistency} is the structural consistency condition: a common latent response generates all potential outcomes and the observed outcome at the realized treatment, in the simultaneous NPSEM-style formulation used for modified treatment policies \citep{DiazWilliamsHoffmanSchenck2023}. The common null set makes the equality simultaneous over doses, which is the form needed when the clamp map evaluates the response at \(d_\delta(A)\).

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:exchangeability}[Conditional exchangeability]
Under \(P^{\mathrm F}\), the treatment \(A\) and latent response variable \(U\) are conditionally independent given \(X\).
\end{assumptionv}

\Cref{obj:ass:exchangeability} is the latent-response randomization condition \citep{DiazWilliamsHoffmanSchenck2023}. Within each stratum, it lets the conditional treatment density weight the same latent-response distribution that determines the structural mean.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_1}[Full-data response mean]
For a full-data law package \(P^{\mathrm F}\) with latent-response carrier \(\mathcal U\), latent variable \(U\), structural maps \(g_x:[0,1]\times\mathcal U\to[0,1]\), and observed stratum \(X\), the full-data response mean in stratum \(x\in\mathcal X\) is
\[
  m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)=\mathbb E_{P^{\mathrm F}}\{g_x(a,U)\mid X=x\},\qquad a\in[0,1].
\]
When the law package is in the full-data continuity model, the map \(a\mapsto m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)\) is continuous on \([0,\bar\delta]\) for every \(x\in\mathcal X\).
\end{definitionv}

The full-data response mean \leanref{sym:m_x_F}{\ensuremath{m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)}} is the fiberwise structural analogue of the observed regression extension. It isolates the average structural response at dose \(a\) among units in stratum \(x\), which is the quantity the bridge result compares with \(\mu_x^P\) on the threshold interval.

\begin{assumptionv}{ass:full-data-response-continuity}[Full-data response continuity]
Under the full-data law \(P^{\mathrm F}\), for every \(x\in\mathcal X\), the full-data response mean \(a\mapsto m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)\) is continuous on \([0,\bar\delta]\).
\end{assumptionv}

\Cref{obj:ass:full-data-response-continuity} is specific to this analysis. It places the continuity requirement directly on the structural response mean over the range where clamping can create a threshold evaluation, matching the interval on which the observed regression extension is used.

These conditions define the full-data causal class and the decision criteria used below.

\begin{definitionv}{def:full-data-model-class}[Full-data causal class \(\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\)]
\(\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\) is the collection of full-data law packages \(P^{\mathrm F}\) satisfying the following memberwise structure relative to \cref{obj:def:model-class,obj:ass:consistency,obj:ass:exchangeability,obj:ass:full-data-response-continuity}.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Latent response space.)} Each member carries its own standard Borel latent-response space \(\mathcal U\) and an \(\mathcal U\)-valued random variable \(U\).
\item \textbf{(Structural response maps.)} Each member carries jointly Borel maps
\[
g_x:[0,1]\times\mathcal U\to[0,1],
\qquad x\in\mathcal X,
\]
and a jointly measurable potential-outcome process
\[
\{Y(a):a\in[0,1]\}.
\]
\item \textbf{(Full-data law.)} Each member carries a probability law for
\[
(X,A,Y,U,\{Y(a):a\in[0,1]\}).
\]
\item \textbf{(Observed margin.)} The \((X,A,Y)\)-margin is some \(P\in\mathcal M\).
\item \textbf{(Causal member conditions.)} Within the member's own carrier, the simultaneous latent-response consistency, latent randomization, and fiberwise structural-mean continuity on \([0,\bar\delta]\) conditions hold.
\end{itemize}
The class is formed member by member, with membership determined on each member's own standard Borel carrier.
\end{definitionv}

The full-data class \leanref{sym:mathcal_M_F}{\ensuremath{\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}}} keeps the observed law inside \(\mathcal M\) while allowing each member to carry its own standard Borel latent-response space. This memberwise formulation is the natural setting for comparing causal and observed decision problems without imposing a common latent carrier across all laws.

\begin{definitionv}{def:causal-clamp-mean}[Causal clamp mean \(\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})\)]
For a full-data law \(P^{\mathrm F}\) as in \cref{obj:def:full-data-model-class}, the clamp policy \(d_\delta\) from \cref{obj:def:clamp-policy}, and \(0\leq\delta\leq\bar\delta\), define the causal clamp mean by
\[
\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})
=
\mathbb E_{P^{\mathrm F}}\!\left[Y(d_\delta(A))\right].
\]
\end{definitionv}

The causal clamp mean \leanref{sym:psi_delta}{\ensuremath{\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})}} is the mean outcome under the same lower-threshold modified treatment policy used to define the observed clamp functional in \cref{obj:def:clamp-functional}. Thus the causal estimand changes the interpretation of the response while preserving the policy rule and threshold range.

\begin{definitionv}{def:causal-frontier-criteria}[Causal frontier criteria \(R_{n,\mathrm F}^\star\) and \(L_{n,\mathrm F}^\star\)]
For the causal clamp mean in \cref{obj:def:causal-clamp-mean}, define the causal minimax absolute-error risk over estimators measurable with respect to the observed sample \(O_1,\ldots,O_n\) by
\[
 R_{n,\mathrm F}^\star
 =
 \inf_{\widehat\psi}\sup_{P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}}
 \mathbb E_{(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n}}
 \left|\widehat\psi-\psi_{\delta_n}(P^{\mathrm F})\right|.
\]
For intervals \(C_n\) measurable with respect to the same observed sample, define the causal minimax honest expected length by
\[
 L_{n,\mathrm F}^\star
 =
 \inf_{C_n:\,\inf_{P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}}
 (P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n}
 \left\{\psi_{\delta_n}(P^{\mathrm F})\in C_n\right\}\geq1-\alpha}
 \sup_{P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}}
 \mathbb E_{(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n}}
 \left[\operatorname{len}(C_n)\right].
\]
\end{definitionv}

The causal minimax risk \leanref{sym:R_n_F_star}{\ensuremath{R_{n,\mathrm F}^\star}} and causal honest-length criterion \leanref{sym:L_n_F_star}{\ensuremath{L_{n,\mathrm F}^\star}} compare observed-sample estimators and intervals under the full-data class through their induced observed-sample behavior. The procedures therefore use the same data as in the observed experiment while the target is the causal clamp mean.

The next proposition gives the identification step. It connects the structural mean under the full-data law to the observed regression extension and then identifies the causal clamp mean with the observed clamp target.

\begin{propositionv}{prop:causal-bridge}[Causal bridge identity]
Let \(P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\) satisfy \cref{obj:def:full-data-model-class}, let \(P\) be its observed \((X,A,Y)\)-margin, and let \(d_\delta(a)=\max\{a,\delta\}\) be the clamp map in \cref{obj:def:clamp-policy}.  Suppose the frontier constants satisfy
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Strata.)} \(J\geq 1\).
\item \textbf{(Smoothness and thinning.)} \(\beta>0\), \(\kappa\geq 0\), and \(L>0\).
\item \textbf{(Density and mass constants.)} \(c_->0\), \(c_-\leq \kappa+1\leq c_+\), \(p_{\min}>0\), and \(p_{\min}\leq 1/J\).
\item \textbf{(Threshold and coverage levels.)} \(0<\bar\delta<1\) and \(0<\alpha<1/2\).
\end{itemize}
For \(x\in\mathcal X\) and \(a\in[0,1]\), define the full-data response mean
\[
m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)
=
P^{\mathrm F}(X=x)^{-1}
\int_{\{X=x\}} g_x(a,U)\,dP^{\mathrm F}.
\]
Then, for every \(x\in\mathcal X\) and every Borel set \(B\subseteq[0,1]\),
\[
P(X=x)^{-1}
\int_{\{X=x,\ A\in B\}} Y\,dP^{\mathrm F}
=
\int_B m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)\pi_x(a)\,da.
\]
Moreover, for every \(x\in\mathcal X\) and every \(a\in[0,\bar\delta]\),
\[
m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)=\mu_x^P(a).
\]
Consequently, for every \(\delta\in[0,\bar\delta]\), the following identities hold:
\[
d_\delta(a)\in \operatorname{supp}\{\pi_x(a)\,da\text{ on }[0,1]\}
\quad
\text{for each }x\in\mathcal X\text{ and for conditional-treatment-a.e. }a,
\]
\[
Y(d_\delta(A))
=
Y\mathbf 1\{\delta<A\}+Y(\delta)\mathbf 1\{A\leq\delta\}
\quad P^{\mathrm F}\text{-a.s.},
\]
and, with
\[
\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})=\int Y(d_\delta(A))\,dP^{\mathrm F},
\qquad
\nu_\delta(P)=\int Y\mathbf 1\{\delta<A\}\,dP,
\qquad
q_x(\delta)=\int_0^\delta \pi_x(a)\,da,
\]
the causal clamp mean equals the observed clamp target
\[
\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})
=
\nu_\delta(P)+\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}p_xq_x(\delta)\mu_x^P(\delta)
=
\theta_\delta(P).
\]
\end{propositionv}

\Cref{obj:prop:causal-bridge} is the fiberwise bridge. Conditional exchangeability converts observed outcomes within a stratum and treatment set into an integral of the structural response mean against the treatment density; continuity and the observed regression extension then identify that mean on the threshold interval. The final identity shows that the causal lower-threshold policy mean is exactly the clamp functional analyzed in the observed experiment.

A second structural fact records that the full-data class covers the observed model class exactly at the margin. The construction uses regular conditional distributions on standard Borel spaces, a standard measurable-disintegration tool \citep[Theorem 6.3]{Kallenberg2002Foundations}.

\begin{propositionv}{prop:observed-margin-surjectivity}[Observed margin surjectivity]*
Fix \(J\in\mathbb N\) and constants \(\beta,\kappa,L,c_-,c_+,p_{\min},\bar\delta,\alpha\in\mathbb R\). Assume:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(Regime constants.)} \(0<J\), \(0<\beta\), \(0\leq \kappa\), \(0<L\), \(0<c_-\), \(c_-\leq \kappa+1\), \(\kappa+1\leq c_+\), \(0<p_{\min}\), \(p_{\min}\leq 1/J\), \(0<\bar\delta<1\), and \(0<\alpha<1/2\).
\item \textbf{(Observed model.)} \(P\in\mathcal M\) means that \(P\) satisfies \cref{obj:def:model-class} with constants \((\beta,\kappa,L,c_-,c_+,p_{\min})\).
\item \textbf{(Full-data model.)} \(P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\) means that \(P^{\mathrm F}\) satisfies \cref{obj:def:full-data-model-class} with constants \((\beta,\kappa,L,c_-,c_+,p_{\min},\bar\delta)\).
\end{itemize}
Then, for every \(P\in\mathcal M\), there exists \(P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\) whose observed \((X,A,Y)\)-margin is exactly \(P\). Hence the observed-margin map from \(\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\) onto \(\mathcal M\) is surjective.

Moreover, for every \(n\in\mathbb N\) and every \(\delta\in[0,\bar\delta]\), the causal frontier criteria in \cref{obj:def:causal-frontier-criteria} satisfy
\[
R_{n,\mathrm F}^{\star}(\delta)=R_n^\star(\delta)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
L_{n,\mathrm F}^{\star}(\delta,\alpha)=L_n^\star(\delta,\alpha).
\]
Here \(R_n^\star(\delta)\) is the infimum, over observed-measurable estimators with values in \([0,1]\), of the worst-case absolute-error risk over \(P\in\mathcal M\) for the observed clamp target at threshold \(\delta\), and \(L_n^\star(\delta,\alpha)\) is the infimum, over uniformly honest observed-measurable confidence procedures at level \(1-\alpha\), of the worst-case expected interval length over \(P\in\mathcal M\).
\end{propositionv}
% CAUSALSMITH-CITED-SCOPE-BEGIN prop:observed-margin-surjectivity
\verificationfootnotetext{\textbf{Formalization scope.} This result uses the published conclusion \citep{Kallenberg2002Foundations} (Theorem 6.3 (conditional distribution)). The cited source proof is not formalized here: Lean verifies its use and all remaining steps. If that cited conclusion is false, the portions of this result that depend on it are not certified.}
% CAUSALSMITH-CITED-SCOPE-END prop:observed-margin-surjectivity

\Cref{obj:prop:observed-margin-surjectivity} establishes the decision-theoretic equivalence needed for causal transport. Every observed law in \(\mathcal M\) has a full-data representative in \(\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\), and the risk and honest-length criteria agree at each fixed threshold. Thus lower bounds and attainable procedures for the observed clamp target can be read as statements about the causal clamp mean on the declared full-data class.

One technical convention remains for comparing expected lengths. The next definition fixes the order-preserving way real bounds are read inside the extended nonnegative reals.

\begin{definitionv}{synth_11}[Real-to-extended embedding]
Under the notation and hypotheses of the objects introduced earlier in the paper, a real number \(t\in\mathbb R\) is embedded into the extended nonnegative reals by
\[
t\longmapsto \max\{t,0\}\in[0,\infty]\;.
\]
The image is finite and equals the positive part of \(t\), so real expected-length bounds are compared with extended-real expected lengths through this embedding.
\end{definitionv}
